## ASSISTANT

Bearings taken. Here's where the project stands and what this session is for.

**Hygiene done:** eb2fcb4e's raw transcript archived and exported, `make archive` swept clean, my session-start line is in the lineage log, all committed with the Session-Id trailer.

**The campaign (DK direction at last wrap): the outline for the first non-throwaway draft.** Every named input is in place, and I've read them:

- **The allocation sheet** — the quantitative skeleton: 21 rows (19a "The Pocket" approved), 29,936 specimen words → 51.4k planned, with a named reserve queue to ~55.8k. The F0–F7 failure ramp is seated in the rows, with the political-ledger thread running F0 → ch. 5 → ch. 16's extracted concession → ch. 19 SPERBER → F7.
- **The rulings, all in:** protagonist **Danish** (Oberstløjtnant Niels Holt, provisional, family in Fredericia), voice **close-third past** with three imported first-person techniques (stake-in-the-room, felt-lateness beat, free-indirect unsaid) as per-chapter requirements. 19a approved, ch. 16 concession coupled, Marei perimeter confirmed.
- **The craft rules** (critique-profile §4) — eight standing rules the outline enforces per station: geometry only from the map ledger, epigram ration 1-in-3, no prolepsis, monologue→argument, per-character speech rules, failures charged to named signatures, two-three plants left unpaid, red drafted norm-bound.
- **The atlas** runs clean (`path/flow/critical` all live) and transport-1983 §8 has the story cash-out: Jutland Division's march-table problem on A10/E3, the Great Belt as a *ferry* operation, red's two-crossings-wide neck at Schlutup/Herrnburg, period signage discipline.
- **The red-room texture bank** (oob-verification Addendum 2) is at quote+page grade — the ZZB's staff-culture material (three Germans cleared for the full plan, documents in Russian above division, no lateral consultation, the 30-day warning assumption) is scene-ready.

**What the outline itself must decide or discharge:**

1. **Q3 — F7's antagonist** (who fights the ch. 20 audit): explicitly deferred *into* the outline. The sheet's cheapest-true candidate is the corps' own success-hungry higher echelon.
2. **The Danish-ripple redesign** (matrix-judging §5): the German Kreis geometry (Column 217, the ch. 16 overlay, 19a's school) needs its named ensemble carrier — Roloff or Lammers; Holt's B-plot moves behind the Little Belt; Witzel must actually appear; and the Oberstløjtnant rank-grade question (principal G-3 vs. deputy who inherits the chair) wants settling before scene-grain beats.
3. **The two standing tests the outline is the venue for:** the reader-ahead audit table (independent-take §3.4 — any S1–S3 station that fails demotes to adversarial staging; if S4–S5 also fail, that's Andon territory) and unsettle #2's two-cycles-per-station check (size-and-shape §7). The station/cycle audit in allocation-sheet §3 already passes at row grain — the outline has to hold that at scene grain.
4. **Per-Part axis-and-front-trace maps** drawn from the atlas before any scene beats are written — the countermeasure to the specimen's wrong-border opening.

My read on shape: the outline's unit is the allocation-sheet row, expanded to scene-grain beats — each row carrying its scenes, its engine assignments, its craft-rule constraints, its station/cycle tags, and its front-trace anchor; the reader-ahead table and the Danish-ripple items resolved as we go; Q3 decided where F7's row gets built. Ready to begin wherever you want to start — the Part-I front-trace map and rows 1–8, or the open decisions (rank grade, Kreis carrier, Q3) first.

## ASSISTANT

The outline is built and committed — `planning/outline.md`, 1,142 lines, the drafting contract for the first non-throwaway draft. Here's what you're ratifying.

**How it was built.** Four Opus agents produced scene-grain beat inventories of all 20 specimen chapters (per the standing spend preference — the specimen text itself stayed out of my context); I synthesized the outline from those plus the allocation sheet, the matrix ruling, the craft rules, and atlas queries. Session hygiene ran first: eb2fcb4e's transcript archived, lineage log updated.

**The three delegated decisions, argued in §1:**

1. **F7's antagonist is Oberst Bjelke (DA), COMBALTAP assessments** — the theater officer who adopted the confession wall as a "theater instrument" in ch. 15. His campaign report needs the corps to have been *right* because his own mid-war endorsement is on the line. He's planted voice-only at the Karup conferences before he turns, his motive is self-defense of a good-faith act rather than venality, and F7 becomes S1 recurring one echelon up: victory as the last flattering metric. He also gets the drafting voice's epigram tic on purpose — the polished quotable voice belongs to the man who is wrong.
2. **Roloff carries the German Kreis geometry** (over Lammers): hold orders and overlays are plans work, so the man who drafts the annex that prices his own street is the natural owner; it gives the log-keeper a body; and Lammers' impersonal authority stays clean. Roloff inherits the house in Schleswig, Column 217, the school — and gets exactly one register break, in 19a.
3. **Holt is the deputy who inherited the G-3 chair** — the Oberst-graded principal left with the October rotation, the relief froze in mobilization. Closes the rank flag, makes "no anointed protagonist" structural, and rhymes with the new-commander device: the corps meets the war led by its second-stringers.

**The big structural work is the front rebase (§4).** The specimen fights its entire war backward — red entering at the Danish border. The corrected war enters at the Lübeck/IGB sector and attacks north, and almost all the specimen's terrain furniture survives, because the Schlei–Danevirke narrows always were the peninsula's neck — the Danevirke now faces the way it was built to face. The Treene-line fight becomes the Trave; the Eider valve stays, now with a geometric reason; the pocket's breakout comes northwest through Friedrichsberg exactly where the B-plot geometry already lived. Fine tactical traces carry [TRACE] flags for the per-chapter atlas pass.

**The audits ran and passed:** the reader-ahead table (§6) rules all stations PASS — the specimen's middle-third break is repaired by staging ch. 14's re-audit as on-page process and converting ch. 13's answer into the staff's wrong answers. The cycle test (§7) discharges unsettle #2 at outline grain. The F-ramp is seated with named signatures, three of them Holt's own (the F3 wrong-shoulder release over Roloff's objection, the F5 calibration ruling, 19a's misread endorsement).

**One thing I owe you before you read anything else: the allocation sheet's arithmetic is wrong.** Its 21 row targets sum to **49.25k, not the stated 51.4k** — and under its own slip precedent that drafts out at ~42–44k, below the floor. The outline proposes the honest correction (+0.25k to the five rows whose specified scene-lists genuinely carry more → 50.5k planned, true reserve ceiling ~54.2k) and prints the original targets pending your ruling.

**For your ruling with the outline:** the §1 decisions, the arithmetic fix, and the provisional names (Holt, Merete, Bjelke). After ratification, the path is a fresh draft one from this contract, with each chapter's map pass before its prose.

## ASSISTANT

**Draft one exists.** Twenty-one chapters, ~40,000 words, committed chapter by chapter and pushed to the remote. Session wrapped: lineage log, entity index row 8, archive run, status.md updated for the successor.

**What's in it.** The full outline executed: the war now opens where it must — across the IGB at the Lübeck sector, with the Danevirke facing south the way it was built to — and the specimen's best furniture (the valve, the narrows, the confession wall, the two-clock symmetry) survives the inversion intact. Holt is Danish close-third throughout, the deputy who inherited the chair; Witzel finally exists, in eight chapters, and turned out to be one of the draft's best voices ("If the capitals wish to purchase confidence from this headquarters, they will at least receive an itemized bill"). The F-ramp is fully seated with named signatures — three of the seven failures carry Holt's own initials, including F3's wrong-shoulder fires over Roloff's flat objection and F5's crest calibration, which now bites *inside* ch. 19's execution: the door closes five hours late and Loheide pays the difference. Bjelke enters at Karup in ch. 15 exactly as designed, and the ch. 20 audit fight lands the F7 argument with him unbeaten but outlasted. The Zealand brigades cross by ferry. The epigram ration held at 6 of 21; I caught and cut two prolepsis violations and one shared-coinage voice bleed in-session.

**The honest accounting.** The draft is **−21% against the ratified 50.5k plan**, and the cause is the documented one: the 30k attractor operated on *me*, in real time, exactly as the critique profile predicted — every chapter's first pass landed 25–40% under its floor, and it took one to two deliberate expansion passes per chapter (each committed separately, so the diffs show the mechanism) to recover Part I to −5..−15%. Part III sits thinner because I prioritized completing the skeleton over perfecting each row. This is a measured deficit with a per-chapter map, not a silent drift — it's flagged in status.md as the next campaign's opening condition.

**What I'd suggest next**, per the process rules: you read draft one and name thinness specifically — the human diagnoses, and your read may find that some "deficit" is actually the right length while other passages that hit their floors are thin in ways word counts can't see. Then the thickening campaign runs row-by-row against the outline's scene lists, with the reserve queue (Vestergaard window, Karup interleaf, the fourth red beat pending your ruling) available for the true gaps. One catch from the build also stands for your review: the allocation sheet's stated 51.4k was a summation error — the real plan was 49.25k, corrected to 50.5k under your ratification.

Both done and pushed.

**The reading apparatus:** `make pdf` builds `build/the-mission.pdf` — 146 pages at 5.5×8.5in, TeX Gyre Pagella, each chapter on a fresh page, with a draft stamp (date + commit hash) on the title page so you always know which state you're reading. `make manuscript` gives the single-file Markdown, and `make wordcount` prints the per-chapter table against the plan. Deliberately reading-grade per your note — the WB-style production assembly stays for later, and the Makefile says so.

**The Part III expansion:** all seven rows thickened ahead of your specific feedback. Part III moves from −31% to −17% against its floors; the manuscript stands at **42.1k** (from 40.0k). What went in, briefly: ch. 15 stages the Karup conversion exchanges and the observables-not-claims discipline ("a claim is a hope with a format"); ch. 16 stages Hvidt negotiating the concession to its floor on the record; ch. 17 gets Zawadzki's three questions into the folder and the idling-machine frame around the Rahn call; ch. 18 adds Loheide's hourly rate report and the night passage-of-lines at the marrying points; ch. 19 itemizes F5's tuition at the drainage cut (nothing fails — the bill just arrives with three named signatures on it); ch. 19a gives the school its defense in the supply lieutenant's own report format, plus the Territorials' second war and the turned-around battalions ("the corps' last reserve was its soldiers' obedience, and it was spent knowingly"); ch. 20 adds the declined communiqué request and Bjelke's concession-method in the audit fight.

The remaining gap to plan is ~8.3k, now spread fairly evenly rather than concentrated in Part III. Your read is the right next instrument for it — the deficits I can still close are arithmetic, but at this point the question of *where* the book is actually thin (versus where 2.0k is simply the chapter's right size) is the diagnosis the process rules reserve for you. I'm ready to work from your named findings — and curious what the challenges-at-40k-that-weren't-there-at-30k are; if they're what I'd guess (rhyme density and the wall's ubiquity compounding with length), those are outline-level conversations worth having before the next expansion round.

**Done since your message:**
- **Tags:** `draft-zero` (at the specimen's completion commit, annotated non-canonical) and `draft-one` (at the 42.1k state you're reading) — both pushed. Your reading copy is now frozen; main moves freely underneath it.
- **Protocol:** granular feedback it is, logged in `notes/dk-read-log.md` (your two findings so far are its first entries, triaged). I'm continuing in this session rather than forcing a boundary.
- **Profile:** your tic observation is now critique-profile §5.2 — the diffable finding is that *expansion-pass* prose concentrates the tics far above first-pass rates; under length pressure the model reaches hardest for its signature moves. The scrub will weight the expansion diffs, and this tranche's additions carried the budget consciously (you'll find them lighter on negation-definitions and "like a man" — not clean, but lighter).
- **Length:** 42.1k → **44.1k**. The whole middle band got its round — chs. 12 (the ninety minutes reconstructed in journal time), 11 (Zawadzki's signed sentence), 14 (the sergeant's margin — the audit-as-process now turns on a physical document), 8 (the bridge-or-seniority file), 13, 10, 9, 7, 6.

**One perimeter flag for you:** ch. 7 now carries a *narrated* letter from Merete — contents summarized in indirect discourse, no quoted text, no epistolary scene. I read that as the letter-equivalent of the allowed narrated call; if it's outside the Marei-perimeter ruling as you meant it, say the word and it comes out (it's one self-contained paragraph).

**What remains on length:** ~6.4k to plan. Most chapters now sit −8 to −16%; the stubborn tier is 18/19/19a at −22..−25% after two passes each — a third same-session pass risks diminishing returns. The remaining levers, in my preferred order: (1) your named thinness, which will land where word counts can't see; (2) the reserve queue's formal draws — the Vestergaard and Karup units are pre-justified, though my 12/15 expansions already delivered part of their content, so I'd draw them only where your read confirms the want; (3) **the fourth red beat (~0.9k, Kuragin's own note northward) still needs your ruling** — it's the one queue item flagged as breaking R3's three-beats design, and it would incidentally close a fifth of the gap. No action needed until you get there in the read.

## USER

"photograph of a dance": this is still quite good, but I remember an earlier version of this being more explicit about not seeing where the weight is distributed in the momement or where it's going; I don't think Hvidt would unpack the metaphor, even this early in his tenure, but I think he would pack more into it.

"The readiness measures began the same week": I'm cautiously in favor of the understated slide into war, but want to make sure this is intentional and not due to a mis-shuffling of material. We may want notes on the (timing of) information that Hvidt has on the threat environment and red intentions, even if it doesn't land in the text.

Chapter 2 ending on a dash?

For pathos/narm calibration, the Territorial battalion east of Bad Oldesloe stuff lands on the right side of the line. Good and affecting.

Chapter 4 jump to red perspective is not too jarring as-is, and I imagine this will be even smoother when we add paratext.

Haven't gotten to the Merete letter or fourth red beat yet, but you have my general approval on these. The guidance around Merete was to keep B-plot from _just_ being letters and calls, and I think we're in a good place for this. And there's no hard requirement to keep to three red beats, and we can assess based on whether it works.

Hell of a phrase asking to be quoted in "read reports from the bottom, which was where he had been raised". I think we broadly earn these from the earnestness of the genre and from it being aligned with Rylski's speech/personality. But I'll be curious to see what our review agents think of such.

I'm liking Rylski more and more as I hear him speak. (The conceit, which doesn't need to enter the text, here is more or less that the author of the text has had drinks with Rylski, or officers like him from which he is the composite, after the Cold War has ended however it ended in this setting? He seems a bit outside the register that the blue characters would know how to write.)

"The ratio has a pedigree" paragraph could use rewriting. Several tics. Some of its assertions might be moved into the dialogue that follows, to be arrived at more naturally?

Kuragin, in a new office, writes the operation's report north; Rylski supplies true figures and watches his own reconciliation change function — the document built to make the operation honest becomes the proof the operation was warned, and the tidier his columns, the better the exhibit. Zawadzki goes north in the second week and the Front receives an acting commander; nobody says why; Rylski finds there is no column for it. Batch 3 applied, committed, pushed. The answers in order:

**"Decides the campaign"** — it was over-claiming: an oracular self-caption in a character's mouth (the specimen had it as narrator prolepsis; I'd moved the sentiment rather than fixed it). Now Aakjær grounds the stakes in the one consequence he actually owns: *"Until I can say which, I cannot tell the corps what its reserve is for — spending it against the first enemy is prudence, and against the second it is suicide a week early."*

**Radio discipline** — honest answer: my general knowledge says assembly-phase emission control was documented WP practice (silence itself a NATO warning indicator), but the shelf holds only *adjacent* evidence — Glantz's maskirovka radio-deception and the ZZB's courier-and-no-lateral-consultation culture — not a direct extraction. So: concordance row opened as VERIFY for the doctrine pass, and the line rewritten to be safe under either finding — Aakjær now treats silence as *expected* doctrine rather than a lapse he's lucky about, and his promise shifts to the part that's analytically sound regardless: "silence kept by forty thousand men has a shape — it has edges... and edges can be plotted."

**The filed-paper tic** — cut clean; the plant works better unnarrated.

**The exchange rate** — you caught a real muddle, and it was mine, not the characters'. The specimen's line contradicted its own next sentence: "positions for hours" claims delay is being bought, then "we are not delaying them" denies it. The rewrite resolves it in exact units, all observable from Loheide's chair: one prepared position (with most of its company) buys roughly one hour of red's march table; the October exercise assessed the same trade at one-for-four; and — the part the liaison is instructed to say slowly — the hours sold don't register as hours red is *losing*, because his advance rate hasn't varied since the border: the belts were already in his budget. That's the correct pivot for what the chapter teaches (the exercise's flattering exchange rates, and the difference between delaying a man and merely making his punctuality expensive), and Loheide can observe every term of it.

**And the concordance doc exists:** `notes/wargame-concordance.md`, eleven rows mapping every load-bearing quantitative claim in the draft to its instrument or bench source, each graded CONSISTENT / TEXT-LED / VERIFY — with a standing sweep rule in both directions (any wargame campaign closing sweeps the table; any revision touching a listed passage checks its source still stands). The ch. 4 exchange is row 1, with its 1:1-vs-1:4 *ratio* honestly flagged TEXT-LED: the instrument gives the screen's cost and red's tempo insensitivity but never emitted a position-per-hour figure, so if a future calibration makes the screen cheap or red's tempo belt-sensitive, the table flags exactly which passages it orphans — ch. 4's pivot and ch. 1's exercise callback both listed. Worth noting the happy row too: F5's fourteen-hours-wrong crest is CONSISTENT *by design* — it's the campaign-3 meta-claim dramatized, so no recalibration can reverse it without reversing the finding the book exists to carry.

## ASSISTANT

Sharp question, and it opens onto exactly the modeling seam you name. Let me answer in layers, then wire the answer into the record:

**Is he right?** In the period-doctrine sense, yes: against a Soviet-style deliberate attack, artillery is the dominant casualty producer, and suppression genuinely buys loss-rate relief — the liaison's claim is what any competent division staff of 1983 would certify. But notice its epistemic status *inside the book's own rules*: it's the division's certified belief, and **nobody ever audits it**. Loheide's ch. 9 conversion ("an hour of his guns' silence is worth three hours of my men's lives") is exactly the kind of number the wall exists to interrogate, and it never gets the treatment — because the corps never purchases the hour retail, so there's never an observed column to post against it.

**The framing doesn't mean counter-battery is immaterial — it means it was unpurchasable at the market's then-prices.** That's F1's actual content: the corps could afford deep or close, not both, and the currencies in use couldn't even express the liaison's request. The debt does get paid — wholesale, once, at the door: ch. 19's program opens with counter-preparation on the gun lines and the first air wave onto the gun groupings, at the one hour red's guns are poorest. And the payment is *confounded* — red's artillery was past its crest anyway, so even in success the corps can't isolate what suppression bought. The claim enters the war believed and leaves it believed, never measured. I think that's the honest texture, and I'll make the payoff legible with one restrained clause in ch. 19.

**And yes — this is the no-explicit-fires modeling choice surfacing in the text.** The instrument treats fires organically inside combat resolution and consumption; it has never adjudicated a suppression-efficacy claim, so every fire-causal assertion in the draft (the liaison's hour, the 2/3 figure, the 80–140/hour meter's sensitivity to suppression) is TEXT-LED, standing on doctrine rather than the model. If a future calibration layer models fires explicitly, it could break either way: suppression-cheap would weaken F1's tragedy (the corps *could* have bought the hour); suppression-ineffective would collapse ch. 9's "both correct." Which is to say: this is itself an instance of the meta-claim — one more case of *whose consumption model is the argument standing on*. Logging it as its own concordance row now:

> *The program's second serial was counter-battery, entire: the hunted hour a muddy liaison officer had asked for on the war's second morning. It had never been for sale by the sortie or the shell. The corps delivered it now wholesale, at the one hour it could afford to, against guns already going poor — and whether the hunting or the poverty did the work, no instrument in the theater would ever be able to say.*

The last clause is the load-bearing one — it keeps the payoff from becoming a vindication. The liaison's request is honored *and* the question of whether he was right is explicitly left unmeasurable, which is the honest reading of a war where fires were never a modeled subsystem and the one experiment ran confounded. If the callback itself feels like one rhyme too many, the first two sentences can go and the "whether the hunting or the poverty" clause can survive alone — it works without the callback.

GMSD screen, Polish Küstenfront +2 days); Danevirke faces south; Treene-line fight → Trave; Eider valve (Friedrichstadt/Sorgbrück/Nordfeld) serves west-route Jyske march; Sehestedt canal crossing = red salient; pocket breakout northwest through Friedrichsberg. [TRACE] flags resolve at per-chapter atlas passes (`python3 -m atlas path/flow/critical`).
   - F-ramp F0–F7 charged to named signatures, three to Holt (NH): F3 wrong-shoulder fires over Roloff's objection (ch. 12), F5 crest calibration on Rylski's generous three-fifths (ch. 18, bites in ch. 19 — door closes 5 hours late), F6 readjustment endorsement over Roloff's pinned sheet (19a).
   - Political ledger thread: F0 deferred DSB Great Belt ferry requisition (ch. 2) → guidance line (ch. 5) → extracted concession/battalion raid (ch. 16) → SPERBER refusal vs capitals' appetite (ch. 19) → F7 audit fight vs Bjelke (ch. 20).
   - Craft law (outline + critique-profile §4): no prolepsis; no education/lesson/trap vocabulary; epigram ration = narrator curtains only chs. 1, 5, 10, 13, 17, 20; em-dash ≤10/ch; per-character speech rules; red norm-bound; unpaid plants (1978 photo, signal-lamp lieutenant, Kuragin's note northward); misfired symmetry (Güstrow's tidier wall lies).
   - Reading protocol: DK reads frozen `draft-one` git tag (5e5986a, 42.1k, 146pp PDF); main moves freely; findings logged in notes/dk-read-log.md with triage MECHANICAL/REVISION/DESIGN/VERIFY/CALIBRATION.
   - Concordance discipline: notes/wargame-concordance.md (12 rows) maps text claims ↔ instrument/bench sources, statuses CONSISTENT/TEXT-LED/VERIFY, bidirectional sweep rules.
   - Standing revision rules accumulated from DK batches: (a) every dialogue scene carries place, hour, third-party presence; (b) when a speech needs "he let that stand"/"he turned" inside it, split the speech and give the room a move; (c) narrator may not foreclose postwar contact between survivors (research conceit: the author has, in effect, had drinks with Rylski after the war); (d) pathos calibration fixed point = Bad Oldesloe Territorial battalion beat ("right side of the line"); (e) period-vocabulary Ngram checks (e.g., "scheduled event" → "an item on his march table").
   - Provenance: every commit carries `Session-Id: 52662a0d-15b0-4e2c-8893-8cf5b0da01f1` trailer + Co-Authored-By Claude Fable 5; make archive hygiene; holdings/ content never copied into repo.
   - Build: `make pdf` (pandoc, xelatex, book class, 5.5×8.5in, 0.75in margin, 11pt, TeX Gyre Pagella, --top-level-division=chapter, draft stamp = date + short hash); `make wordcount`; build/ gitignored.

     ```
     Somebody at the fire-support desk let out a breath through
     his teeth. It was not loud. The room was quiet enough to
     make it a statement, and Hvidt found its author without
     appearing to look.
     ...
     The breath's author studied his desk. Hvidt turned to Holt,
     and his voice changed gear, rebuke to tasking.
     ```
   - Ch. 19 counter-battery payoff clause (DK-approved as written): "The program's second serial was counter-battery, entire: the hunted hour a muddy liaison officer had asked for on the war's second morning. It had never been for sale by the sortie or the shell. The corps delivered it now wholesale, at the one hour it could afford to, against guns already going poor — and whether the hunting or the poverty did the work, no instrument in the theater would ever be able to say."
   - Ch. 4 rewritten liaison exchange (the precision fix): one prepared position ≈ one hour of march table vs exercise 1:4; "the hours we sell him were already in his plan"; observable via unvaried advance rate.

We may want notes on the (timing of) information that Hvidt has on the threat environment and red intentions, even if it doesn't land in the text. / Chapter 2 ending on a dash? / For pathos/narm calibration, the Territorial battalion east of Bad Oldesloe stuff lands on the right side of the line. Good and affecting. / Chapter 4 jump to red perspective is not too jarring as-is... smoother when we add paratext. / Haven't gotten to the Merete letter or fourth red beat yet, but you have my general approval on these. The guidance around Merete was to keep B-plot from _just_ being letters and calls... no hard requirement to keep to three red beats, and we can assess based on whether it works. / Hell of a phrase asking to be quoted in 'read reports from the bottom, which was where he had been raised'... I'll be curious to see what our review agents think of such. / I'm liking Rylski more and more... (The conceit... is more or less that the author of the text has had drinks with Rylski, or officers like him from which he is the composite, after the Cold War has ended however it ended in this setting? Not vehicles--positions for hours.': this is a pretty pivotal moment, so let's make sure it lands correctly. I'm not sure about the units on the latter exchange rate. Is this what the liaison officer meant to say? What the general meant to convey? What you meant to convey? I think most of the characters involved are somewhat confused at this point, but we should make sure that we aren't. If this rests on an insight that fell out of the wargames, we should make sure it is [a] conclusion that the general is in a position to observe, and make sure that we have a doc somewhere that handles concordance, in case we make revisions to the wargaming that reverses this phenomenon."
   - (/context output) + Batch 4: "Is the liaison officer 'right' about counter-battery? The framing seems to suggest that this would be immaterial. Does this relate to the question of not explicitly modeling artillery fires in the wargaming (in favor of treating it organically)?"

Nothing is wrong with the scene, but such considerations point to ways to add specificity, weight, interest, etc."

**The blind panel, five Fable readers, fully blind** (each gets only a scratchpad copy of the manuscript, no repo access, no provenance, explicit orders not to explore):

1. **The 11th-grade B student** — imported directly from the White Buffalo playbook: the assigned essay, "what is this book about and did it work for you," written from what a busy 17-year-old actually retained, skimmed, and got confused by.
2. **The veteran** — Bundeswehr Oberst a.D. with late-'70s Schleswig-Holstein staff tours, writing the letter to the author: procedure, hardware, geography, the Danish-German texture, which men he recognizes from life.
3. **The genre reader** — defense analyst and wargamer, forum long-read: operational/logistic arithmetic checked from the text, what the book hand-waves, where it refuses genre goods and whether the refusals work, placed among Hackett/Clancy/Coyle.
4. **The acquiring editor** — the full editorial letter: proportion (underfed/overfed chapters *named*), ensemble distinctness, the domestic thread, line-level tics with quotes, prioritized revision list. This is the one aimed squarely at the remaining length budget.
5. **The hostile critic** — serious outlet, 1,200 words, unpersuaded: schematism, didacticism, characters as delivery mechanisms, competence-as-pornography, borrowed architectures (if they independently spot the book's structural ancestor, that's signal, not noise — they weren't primed with it).

They'll come back over the next little while; when they do, I'll save all five verbatim into `notes/blind-reads/`, write a synthesis with findings triaged into the read-log's categories (MECHANICAL / REVISION / DESIGN), and bring you the cross-panel convergences — on White Buffalo precedent, the scariest findings will be the ones two unrelated personas hit independently.

<result>**What *The Mission* Is Actually About (In My Opinion)**

When I started this book I thought it was going to be a war novel with battles in it, and technically it is, but honestly it is mostly a book about meetings. It takes place in 1983 in Denmark and northern Germany, and the main character is a Danish staff officer named Holt who works at a NATO headquarters when World War III breaks out. I expected tanks and explosions, and there are some, but almost everything happens in an operations room where officers argue about numbers. The weird part is that by the end I think that's the whole point. The book is about how an army can have every number "green" on its charts and still be losing, because it's measuring the wrong things. A retired old general named Rahn asks Holt at a party, basically, "what is your corps FOR?" and Holt can't answer it, and the whole rest of the book is him figuring that out.

The thing that worked best for me was the wall. Partway through the war, the staff starts writing down every prediction they make and then what actually happened next to it, so they can't lie to themselves. Like "ESTIMATE: crossing restored in 24 hours. ACTUAL: 41." The soldiers nickname it the confession wall. That could be boring but it's actually kind of the main character of the book. There's a scene near the end where Holt's own decision goes wrong (he sends artillery fire to the west side when the real attack was coming on the east side, and a battalion loses its position by three minutes, the report literally says fires granted 1213, position lost 1210) and he has to stand in front of the wall he built while it basically convicts him. I didn't expect a book about spreadsheets to make me feel bad for a guy, but it did.

I'll be honest about what I skimmed. There's a whole chapter about a morning conference where three groups bid for airplanes like it's a fish market, and another one about ferry schedules, and I read those the week we had our tournament so they kind of blurred together. I also had trouble telling some of the officers apart. There's Lammers (supplies guy?), Kjeldsen (air force guy), Witzel (paperwork guy), and Aakjær, the sarcastic intelligence officer, and for the first hundred pages I mixed up which was which because they're all colonels with hard names. Aakjær ended up being my favorite though — he has a speech about how his mother-in-law lives eleven minutes from a beach the enemy might invade, and her whole "evacuation plan" is a government pamphlet that says fill the bathtub, and she keeps refilling it weekly because she already lived through one occupation. That detail stuck with me more than most of the battles. Also confusing: the chapters that switch to the enemy side, with a Polish general (Zawadzki, I think) and his chief of staff Rylski. At first I didn't get why they were there, but their story mirrors the main one — Rylski proves with fuel receipts that their army is five days behind schedule, and at the very end he gets rewarded for his honest bookkeeping by having it used as evidence against his own commander, which is genuinely dark.

The most powerful scene for me was small. Early in the war, a message comes in from some middle-aged reservists defending a bridge, asking which headquarters they're actually dying for, "respectfully, sirs, decide." The book keeps doing that — reminding you that the numbers are people. Holt's coworker Roloff has to process the evacuation paperwork for the column that contains his own wife and kids, and he just initials it and keeps working, and Holt says nothing except confirming the column arrived safely. Nobody cries or gives a speech. I think the author trusts you to feel it, which mostly works, although sometimes I wished someone WOULD give a speech, because the emotional stuff is so buried you can miss it if you're reading fast (which, sometimes, I was).

So did it work for me? Mostly yes, with reservations. It's slow, the names are a workout, and if you skim you get lost because the plot is basically logistics. There's no single villain and honestly not that much "action" for a war book. But the ending earns it. The final report the corps writes says "We were not right. We were solvent" — meaning they never actually predicted anything correctly, they just kept honest books about being wrong and paid their debts on time. That's a strange moral for a war novel, but it feels more true than "the good guys were smarter." I don't know if I'd reread it, but I've thought about the confession wall during actual school assignments, like when I tell myself an essay is "basically done." Scheduled: Tuesday. Actual: not yet achieved. So I guess the book worked.</result>

<result>**REVIEW: *The Mission* (unpublished manuscript, ~60k words) — WWIII on the Baltic flank, fought with a grease pencil**

A friend slipped me this draft with the warning "it's not Team Yankee." He undersold it. This is a Third World War novel about LANDJUT — the Danish-German corps at Rendsburg that every one of us who ever ran the Northern Flank on a map has treated as an off-board holding box while the real game happened in the Fulda Gap. The author has written the box.

**What it is**

November 1983 — KAL 007 autumn, the Bonn marches, Pershing deployment pending — and a Warsaw Pact conventional offensive up the neck of Jutland. The protagonist, Oberstløjtnant Niels Holt, is the corps G-3, and the book never once leaves the operational level. The opening chapter is an ENDEX: a captain razoring nine days of exercise chinagraph off the plexiglass, and a departing German general asking four questions the scoring system has no columns for ("The forty minutes at Hohn... I asked what it *cost*"). That cold open is the whole book in miniature. The war, when it comes, is the exercise's questions collecting their debts.

And here I have to name the thing nobody else on this forum will: this is Goldratt's *The Goal* in field gray. The bottleneck (a bridge at Friedrichstadt passing 290 vehicles an hour with eleven-second gaps of empty plank), the mentor who refuses to answer (retired General Rahn, an unapologetic Jonah figure — "Name the good. One."), the constraint that moves the moment you relieve it (plank-seconds, then shells, then sleep), even the explicit "what is this corps *for*" catechism. The mentor-who-only-asks-questions is a genre trick and I'm naming it. It also *works*, because the author does what Goldratt never had to: he charges the protagonist for the theory. More below.

**Checking the arithmetic**

I did what I always do — ran the numbers the text lets you run — and the manuscript survives it, which almost none of the genre does.

The mobilization race is the real GDP shape: 6th Panzergrenadier at 51 hours, Jutland Division at 90, enemy attack at H+66, division actually closed at H+180. The great scene in Chapter 3 — Lammers pointing at "ELEMENTS 1 JYSKE BRIGADE (RECCE SQN, ADV PARTY) CROSSED LILLEBÆLT 072240" as "the war's first honest sentence" while the depot intake boards glow green — is the best dramatization of the input-metrics fallacy I've read anywhere, fiction or JOPES. Brauer's 1,100 tons for forty minutes of massed fires pencils out to about 1.5 rds/min/tube across a two-division corps' artillery — fine. The bridge throughput (290/hr to 410 at half spacing, claimed "forty percent" gain) is internally consistent. The Sea's Schedule sheet — amphib window closing 8–12 December, port restoration ≥10 days, decision space 7–11 days — foots exactly against its own dates. The casualty meter's two denominations (80–140/hour; "a company's fighting strength every ninety minutes") roughly reconcile. Civil twilight at 0742 in late November at Schleswig's latitude: correct, and I checked. One ding: Aakjær's "the moon is dark on the twenty-seventh" — my almanac makes 27 Nov 1983 a last-quarter moon, rising before the pre-dawn H-hour it matters for. Somebody verify.

The scene where Hvidt interrogates the 1:4.2 exchange ratio down to its 1979 umpire-calibrated correction factor — "And this morning's umpires are where?" — should be laminated and issued to every J2 on earth.

Where the book leans on invention, it mostly says so. The load-bearing premise — that the whole Pact operation forecloses without a working Baltic port before the December gales — is the manuscript's one big gimme. The western Baltic doesn't "close" in any hard sense; the amphib-window argument is defensible, the sea-as-creditor rhetoric pushes past it. The text half-knows this: the after-action audit admits the sea's schedule was wrong by four days. The Great Belt ferry lift in week three also crosses more unmolested than Baltic Fleet air should permit, though the book has at least burned the enemy's air effort down plausibly by then.

**The other side of the hill**

Here's where the manuscript beats its 1980s ancestors on published-since evidence: the enemy is the **Polish Coastal Front** — Zawadzki and his chief of staff Rylski — with a Soviet theater representative sitting silently in the corner, which is what the actual Pact planning documents (the ones we got after 1991) say the Denmark job looked like. Hackett and Clancy gave us monolithic Soviets; this gives us Rylski reconstructing his Front's true positions *backward from fuel consumption* because "a tank that reports its position optimistically still burns diesel from where it actually is." Chapter 11, where he proves his own Front five days behind and his commander declines to receive the conclusion, is Peters' *Red Army* territory and holds its own there.

The best intel beat in the book is Chapter 14: Aakjær discovering there never were echelons — "I mistook a traffic jam for a doctrine. That is not an army forming in waves. That is an army arriving in a queue." That's a real analytic controversy of the period (the echelonment-versus-flow argument) dramatized better than the professional literature ever managed. And the paired nuclear chapters — NATO's locked folder walked once and shut (Ch. 6), Kuragin's folder considered for eleven minutes and refused *on logistical grounds* ("the prize is downwind of the means") in Chapter 17 — are the most honest treatment of theater release I've seen in fiction. Both refusals are bookkeeping, not sentiment. That's correct, and it's chilling in a way mushroom-cloud chapters never are. One loose thread: a two-line report of "unconfirmed employment" in the CENTAG sector appears mid-book and is never resolved. Bold background or dropped stitch — the revision needs to decide.

**What it refuses, and whether the refusals work**

Be warned: there is not one scene inside a tank. No cockpit, no CIC, no sonar shack. Hardware is nearly noun-free — no Leopards, no T-72s, just "tonnage" and "tubes," which is authentically how a G-3 sees it and will cost the book half its natural audience. Combat arrives as *formats*: the Heimatschutz company at Reinfeld asking "which of you we are dying for, respectfully, sirs, decide"; the hinge company's entire battle rendered as an eleven-line radio log ending in HOLDING, then silence ("The silence was not the end of the company. It was the end of the radio"). I have never seen the genre do this, and it hits harder than any autoloader porn I've read. The tension instruments are countdowns and meters — the counter-battery count dropping 1,100 → 900 → 640 → 410 as the enemy banks shells for his last throw is genuinely nail-biting, *because the quieter he gets, the closer he is*.

The other thing I've never seen: the decisive instrument is allowed to fail. The wall's model misses the enemy crest by fourteen hours; Holt signs the computation anyway, with the dissent pinned to it, and Loheide's division pays the difference at a posted rate. The error ledger — the F-series, three of seven entries carrying the protagonist's initials — goes into the record over a theater staffer's silky objections, and the final battle of the book is *an editorial fight over an annex*. "We were not right. We were solvent." When did this genre last have the nerve to end there?

**Pacing and gripes**

Chapters 1–3 are a slow burn that rewards patience; 5 (the two-column graph paper), 8 (the bridge), 11, 12 (the narrows — the one conventional battle chapter, fought entirely from the plexiglass, containing Holt's costly wrong call on the fires release), 14, and 17 are superb. The air-market chapters (9, 15) sag slightly on their second trip to Karup. The chapter numbered "19a" sits *before* 19 in the draft — a flash-forward that half-works and half-reads as a collation error. And the manuscript's one systemic flaw: everyone talks the same. Aakjær, Rahn, Hvidt, Witzel, a battalion commander quoting his grandfather's banker in Kolding — every officer in two armies delivers polished aphorisms on demand. By Chapter 16 it's a war between two faculty senates. Sorkin syndrome. Real staffs contain at least one fool, and this book has none on either side.

**Where it sits on the shelf**

Above Hackett for prose, below him for scope. Nowhere near Clancy for hardware and unashamed of it. Alongside Peters for the enemy chapters. Coyle's readers will bounce off it at speed. Its actual nearest relatives aren't in our genre at all — it's van Creveld's *Supplying War* and Goldratt with a plot, the first WWIII novel whose subject is *measurement itself*. The rivet-counters will find maybe five rivets to count, and four of them are correct.

Recommended without reservation for the operationally minded. 8.5/10 as drafted; a voice pass and a decision about 19a gets it to 9.</result>

## ASSISTANT

The panel round is complete — all five reviews are in `notes/blind-reads/` verbatim with a synthesis at `00-synthesis.md`, five mechanical catches are already fixed on main, and everything is pushed.

**The one scare, resolved:** three reviewers read 19a before 19 — but that was my review-corpus build (locale-collated shell glob), not the book. The Makefile's `$(sort)` is C-locale and correct; your PDF has always been in the right order. Useful accident, though: the editor, believing the swap was authorial, argued on the merits that chronology is the right design — "the shock is Holt's initials on the readjustment order, not the sequence of information."

**Mechanical catches applied** (the panel earned its keep in fact-checking alone): Rahn's birth year contradicted between chs. 1 and 13 — now 1901, which the veteran showed is also the year that makes the Kurland allusion work; rail ferries moved to Korsør in ch. 2 (ch. 15 already had it right); the Jutland Division's reservists cut from forty to fifteen thousand; the ch. 15 moon corrected (27 Nov 1983 was last quarter, not dark — the genre reviewer checked an almanac); a verbatim-repeated sentence deduped.

**The headline convergence — all five reviewers, five vocabularies: voice homogenization.** The student can't tell the colonels apart; the genre reviewer calls it "Sorkin syndrome... a war between two faculty senates. Real staffs contain at least one fool, and this book has none"; the editor names the mechanism (every walk-on is a finalist in the same epigram contest) and the prescription (let two or three be inarticulate; re-differentiate Lammers from Kjeldsen; keep Hvidt out of Rahn's register); the hostile critic radicalizes it ("intentions where its people should be"). This is now the #1 revision target, and it subsumes the tic scrub — the editor even supplied counts (nine "superlative-of-the-war" constructions, six heat/click comprehension moments).

**Your White Buffalo prediction held.** The harrowing document isn't the hostile review — it's partly the student (chs. 9 and 15 skim into each other; "sometimes I wished someone WOULD give a speech") — but the hostile review is the best single document about the book's *risk*: "one calibrated pang per chapter, each promptly filed... nothing in this war is lost that cannot be converted into tuition. That is not tragedy. It is a theodicy of process." Both it and the genre review independently identified The Goal ancestry unprimed, and their opposite verdicts on it — "he charges the protagonist for the theory" vs. "a bake-off between management philosophies" — bracket the book's actual bet. Worth reading both whole.

**Design questions now waiting on you** (§5 of the synthesis): the veteran's heavyweight objection that the HQ never displaces ("the wall re-pinned in the rain, three times... a better story, not a worse one"); the Kiel Canal forcing happening between chapters; one ugly corridor moment for the Danish-German fault line; the emotional-ledger question (does the blue side need one uncashable loss?); the CENTAG plant (two independent flags); Rahn's one early wrongness.

**And the length budget got reframed:** the panel wants cuts (14–16 re-derivation, 2–3 overlap, 9/15 market mechanics) sharing the remaining ~2k with funded adds (Merete's letter quoted, a Rylski middle beat, Vestergaard seeded in ch. 3) — so the residual deficits get spent where readers pointed, not spread evenly. Also worth noting: two passages the panel singled out blind — the countermarch apology and the Feldjäger on the furniture — were written in this session's expansion passes, which is the first external evidence the expansion work is holding the bar.

## USER

As a tooling/bookkeeping note, we should work out a sunrise/sunset/tide/holidays/etc. table for the duration of narrative that we can consult rather than having to reason out from scratch. Cheap way to avoid little errors.

I read the reviews (although only once through the literary one, so far!).

I don't think we should swap chronology on just the basis of that response; I fear that that was more motivated reasoning that we had earned from the previous chapters than a great judgment.

For the ferry correction, let's make sure that the fix makes it to the atlas, and that we do appropriate sourcing and ripple fixing if needed. And consider using the atlas for any other flows that came up in passing that we are not sure on (or that we are sure on, as calibration for the atlas!).

Voice and tic work seems like low-hanging fruit now, and if nothing else will let future review rounds weigh in on more interesting stuff.

The sharper lesson is logged in the file's provenance note: the blind genre reviewer "checked and confirmed" my wrong 0742, so **reviewer agreement on a plausible-precision number is not verification**. Bonus finds: red's great effort opens on **First Advent Sunday**; Volkstrauertag is day 6 of the war; the cease-fire lands on St. Nicholas's night. One flag for you: the Baltic is effectively tideless, and the draft says "one tide" twice in Baltic contexts (chs. 16, 19) — idiom or error, your call. Also noted: the Eidersperrwerk (1973) means the lower Eider crossings are *not* tide-governed — a constraint we must not invent.

**Ferry → atlas ripple** — resolved cleanly: the atlas already carried both Great Belt edges correctly with sourced capacities; ch. 2's error was drafting *against* a correct shelf. All five items are resolved and pushed (`92e94d0`, narrative at 48,569, PDF 168 pp):

**"One tide" — recast, not defended.** Both instances became "one night" / "one night's work" (plus "tide windows" → "dark hours" in the raid's cost list). The Baltic genuinely doesn't run tides, so the honest fix was cheaper than researching a defense of the idiom. Unpinned as requested — easy to revert if you disagree.

**Belt lift — attacked materially, yes.** My assessment: a corps-reserve-sized lift on a 19-km strait for 2.5 days can't cross free against an air arm whose reason for existing is that target. But the attack is bounded by three period-real facts: three weeks of attrition, the short overwater dash inside Danish fighter-and-battery geometry, and — the piece that ties it to the book's spine — red's remaining naval air being wanted *over its own dumps* at exactly that moment. Ch. 15 now carries the second-night raid: one ferry straddled in the Halsskov approach, a fire fought out at sea, eleven casualties, the berth fouled two hours — which retroactively gives the lift's existing "two hours behind an honest schedule" its cause. The raiding regiment pays a fifth of its strength; there is no third night. Concordance row 9 closed as TEXT-LED with the reasoning.

**Voice/tic campaign — first tranche done.** The surgical list-driven items: narrator superlatives-of-the-war cut 9 → 3 (dialogue instances like Hvidt's "most useful hour" kept as speech — characters may use the formula, the narrator shouldn't; the duplicate "shortest" pair resolved in Merete's favor), heat/click comprehension cut to the two origin instances (ch. 1 cheekbones, ch. 5 breech), "like a man" cut 5 → 3 (psalms, the wound, and Merete's doorstep line kept). The em-dash bulk pass (30–44/chapter against a budget of 10) is the campaign's next unit — it needs whole-chapter passes and fresh judgment per conversion, so it starts next turn rather than getting crammed in here. One bonus: while in ch. 19 I found and fixed a chronology inversion my own earlier insertion had created (0538 before "until 0512").

**Repo-public — my assessment: good to publish; the switch is yours.** Audited: all tracked files, all seven raw transcript archives (decompressed and scanned), and the *entire git history* (208 commits) for key/token/credential patterns — zero hits. `.envrc` is just `use flake`; `.gitmodules` uses a relative URL, so the private holdings repo stays unexposed (the submodule simply won't resolve for outsiders, by design). The one residual risk is structural, not present: holdings *text* quoted into future transcripts — the pre-commit guard covers files only, so the distill-don't-copy discipline remains the real control. Your email appears in transcripts, but it's already in every commit. Verdict logged in status.md.

**HDv 100/100 — three routes, and B doesn't need to block.** (1) *Free, now:* the CGSC/CARL digital library and DTIC hold 1980s US student monographs comparing HDv 100/100 with FM 100-5 — e.g. ["Defense in Clausewitz's On War and in FM 100-5 and HDv…"](https://cgsc.contentdm.oclc.org/digital/collection/p4013coll2/id/1664/) — which quote the German doctrine at length; fetch-shelf tier, and probably enough to triangulate CP/displacement practice with the held FM 71-100/FM 100-5 and Bogason. (2) The [Bundesarchiv holds the 1973 original](https://www.deutsche-digitale-bibliothek.de/item/7GXWI6KJQY222YR7XE4NTMDZ3HE2FS7D) (Führungsakademie provenance, indexed via Archivportal-D) — reproduction request, slow but certain. (3) German antiquarian market (ZVAB/Booklooker/eBay.de) — sporadic, cheap when it appears, VS-NfD long lapsed; that's your browser tier. So if you sign off on B, the sensible sequence is: I pull the CARL/DTIC tier into the shelf first, draft the displacement scene against it, and the German original upgrades the sourcing whenever route 2 or 3 lands.

Sources: [CARL/CGSC digital library](https://cgsc.contentdm.oclc.org/digital/collection/p4013coll2/id/1664/), [Deutsche Digitale Bibliothek — HDv 100/100 holdings](https://www.deutsche-digitale-bibliothek.de/item/7GXWI6KJQY222YR7XE4NTMDZ3HE2FS7D), [Archivportal-D](https://www.archivportal-d.de/item/PAF4J3GKAAPEUNC5WQYPAD7Y4MKKIVJX?pdf=true&facetValues%5B%5D=context%3DBNVXQ5H3KAB7FOUOSZ42AYKEVZQDANXD&sort=&hitNumber=40&lang=de&offset=20)

Next up on the standing queue, in order: the em-dash bulk pass (worst six chapters first), then de-homogenization, with your next read-log batches processed as they arrive.

<result>I've completed the research-and-distillation task. Here is the compact report.

## What was fetched (into gitignored `reference/pdf/`)
- **`fm-100-15-corps-operations.pdf`** (archive.org, 269 pp) — full US corps CP echelonment chapter. **Important caveat baked into the file: this is the 1996 edition, not period** (it has C²W/DOCC/MSE). I attribute it explicitly as a post-setting proxy.
- Mined two manuals **already on the shelf** rather than re-fetch: `fm-71-100-...-1978` (period-correct US division CPs + a Soviet-CP passage with distances) and `fm-100-5-...-1982` (period capstone; CPs as targets).
- **de.wikipedia Gefechtsstand** — fetched as a German-language *secondary* source for the German CP vocabulary (Hauptgefechtsstand / Rückwärtiger / Reservegefechtsstand / bewegliche Befehlsstelle) and a displacement-cadence figure. It cites **HDv 100/**200**, not 100/100** — flagged as such.

## What failed
- **HDv 100/100 itself** — confirmed not freely available anywhere (only Bundesarchiv/finding aids). This is the central gap.
- **The brief's target monograph** (CGSC p4013coll2 id **1664**, Gertmann-Sude Clausewitz/HDv 100/100 paper) — the id **404s via the CONTENTdm API**; it's stale or moved. Not quoted.
- **DTIC (apps.dtic.mil)** — `.mil` doesn't resolve from this network (403), so no DTIC survivability/SAMS PDFs. SAMS monographs *are* mirrored on the fetchable CGSC CONTENTdm but I didn't positively ID a corps-CP-displacement title this pass.
- **FM 101-5 (1984)** — only the 1997 edition / partial scans were free; period copy not secured.

## Three most load-bearing findings for the displacement scene
1. **A corps main moves rarely, by design.** German secondary doctrine puts small-formation CP displacement at every 6–12 h but *Großverbände* (division/corps) on the order of ~48 h; US doctrine has the corps main preferring a **built-up-area fixed facility** to hide its large signature. Both make *one* mid-campaign displacement realistic rather than constant churn — and the "built-up area / fixed facilities" line quietly supports the Rendsburg-fortress siting (though it's US-general logic, not a German rule).
2. **Continuity is by hand-off, not a standby facility (US model).** There is no separate "alternate CP" in the US corps set — the rear CP *is* the main's alternate; displacement is **echeloned** (cells bound while the rest stays on the air) so the CP is never wholly down. Whether German doctrine instead kept a truly-manned **Reservegefechtsstand** is the sharpest US-vs-German unknown — potentially a scene-turning point.
3. **The comms cost is the trunk, and the 1983 cost is blunt.** Moving the main tears down and re-homes the multichannel/area-signal trunk. The proxy manual's "stay reachable during displacement" capability is **MSE (late-80s)**; in 1983 the command group rides combat-net *radio*, so the honest cost is: the main's trunk is down for the tear-down + road move + re-establish, with command on radio and the alternate until it's back. **Re-establishment time in hours is not on the shelf.**

## Biggest remaining gap
**HDv 100/100 itself.** Everything German in the file is INFERENCE from a secondary source citing the *wrong* manual (100/200) — so displacement cadence, the Haupt/Rückwärtiger/Reserve manning split, and whether the Reservegefechtsstand is a manned standby all remain UNKNOWN in German terms. The file never substitutes US practice for German silently, and its TO-ACQUIRE list leads with HDv 100/100 (plus HDv 100/200, FM 101-5 1984, a period SAMS/DTIC CP study, and Danish field regulations).

The distillation is at `/home/dlk/workspace/the-mission-1986/reference/cp-doctrine.md`. Per instructions I made no commits and edited no other file; `fm-100-15` was added to the gitignored `reference/pdf/` but I did **not** touch `fetch-shelf.py` or `shelf.md` (main session's call whether to register it).</result>

- **Ch. 12 plant:** Witzel's machine, mid-battle, includes the 1400 daily test call to the alternate site in the woods at Hohn — "the worst day of the war was not an exemption." (Hohn is a deliberate callback: the site of ch. 1's forty-minutes-at-Hohn question becomes the corps' war address.)
- **Ch. 13:** Aakjær now grades the compound's exemption from the harassment fire "separately, and less comfortably: a target banked, in that army's economy, against some better hour" — the sparing de-politened, and the move motivated by the corps' own reading of red's economy.
- **Ch. 14 opening:** the fourteenth night's echeloned move — rear HQ and four radio nets carrying command, trunk down 2200–0340, the wall traveling as a photographed, crated, manifested document and re-pinned at Hohn "in its exact old geography... so that the duty officer coming on at 0600 found every number where his hands knew it lived." Rahn's ch. 10 sentence gets its test in text: *the wall was plywood, and the plywood stayed at Rendsburg, and the wall arrived entire.* Timing threads cleanly: after Rahn's visit, before the fifteenth-morning conference — where Aakjær's crate now reads as move residue for free.
- **Ripple:** ch. 16's casemate billet became a rest hut behind the signals vans; ch. 20's two "Rendsburg" metonyms generalized; ch. 20's "photographed twice before it left the cork" now lands as a reprise of the move's discipline rather than a debut.
- **Bookkeeping:** concordance row 13 opened — the 5h40 trunk figure is honestly TEXT-LED (no sourced re-establishment hours exist on the shelf), and the scene is written to survive either answer to the German Reservegefechtsstand question, with a sweep instruction for when HDv 100/100 arrives. The doctrine distillation itself is committed at `reference/cp-doctrine.md`, with its US-proxy-vs-German gaps explicitly graded; the fetched FM 100-15 turned out to be the 1996 edition and is attributed as post-period proxy, never silently substituted.

One correction against myself: the commit message states ch. 14 at 2,246; the measured count is **2,093** (target 2,100 — deficit still closed, but the number was predicted, not measured). That's the third time this session I've written a count before running `wc`, which in this project of all projects is an embarrassing genre of error — I've written it into persistent memory as a standing rule: numbers in commits and reports come from tool output run after the last edit, or they don't get stated.

Queue from here: the em-dash bulk pass (worst six chapters), then de-homogenization, with the veteran's remaining design items (the canal night, the fault-line corridor scene) awaiting your ruling whenever you want to take them up.

## ASSISTANT

**Automation is live, and the em-dash pass is running.** What's in place:

- **`commit-msg` hook** (installed via `make hooks`, committed as `scripts/commit-msg`): any commit touching `drafts/` gets `Wordcount:` and per-chapter `Chapter-wc:` trailers appended automatically — measured from the *staged* content, not the working tree, so the trailer is ground truth for exactly the state being committed. Hand-stated counts in commit prose are now redundant by construction; the machine's number always rides along to contradict me if I predict again.
- **`make counts`**: per-chapter actual vs. ratified floor with deltas and total (floors encoded once, in `scripts/counts.py`). Current output: total 48,913, −1,837 to plan; the biggest remaining deficits are 10 (−294), 7 (−277), 16 (−249), 15 (−214) — the panel-directed funded adds (Merete's letter in 7, Rylski's middle beat, the 14–16 rebalance) map onto exactly those.
- **`make tics`**: the whole tic inventory as one command — em-dashes per chapter, "the way"/"like a man" counts, the superlative and heat/click regexes, negation-definition candidates. First run already earned its keep by catching a bug in my own hex-escape grep (it reported zeros; the literal-character version reports truth).

**Em-dash campaign, wave one:** six agents are working the worst six chapters in parallel (09: 44, 19: 39, 15: 35, 19a: 34, 08: 34, 18: 30 → target ≤12 each), one file per agent, disjoint, no commits. Guardrails are tight: conversion preference ladder (comma / colon / period), functional dashes preserved (dialogue interruptions, ALL-CAPS report formats untouched), the forbidden-tic list explicit (no new similes, negation-definitions, superlatives, or parentheses), word count pinned to ±15, and each must report measured before/after numbers plus its three least-confident lines for my review. When they return I'll run `make tics` for the independent recount, spot-check the diffs, rebuild the PDF, and commit — at which point the new hook stamps its first measured trailer. Wave two (20: 29, 12: 28, 14: 27, and the mid-teens tail) follows the same pattern.

That's a genuine new tell, and you're right that nothing on the list catches it. Let me name it precisely before fixing it.

**What the sentence is doing:** it reports a practice together with its own aggregate vindication — "[practice], because by the [ordinal iteration] the [outputs] were [superlative-adjacent appraisal]." That's the register of a work summary, not narration: it's me describing a workflow's ROI through the character. The tell has three markers: (1) a mental act rendered one notch too literally in the book's accounting idiom ("logged it as a purchase" — nobody logs a feeling); (2) a retrospective universal doing the vindicating ("each order's best paragraphs," "nobody ever called it anything but"); (3) the claim is *asserted by the narrator* rather than cashed in scene. The existing tells are all lexical (dashes, similes, negations, superlatives); this one is structural, which is why it slipped every grep. It's also the micro-form of two known diagnoses: the hostile critic's "the style is an invoice" (macro) and the critique profile's "double delivery" — because the chapter then *dramatizes* exactly what the sentence pre-claims (the bottle-pours instruction going in verbatim "because it was better than the order"). The sentence spends the payoff before the scenes earn it.

Checking where else the pattern lives before fixing:

**The tell is structural, which is why the list missed it.** Every existing tell is lexical — a grep can find a dash, a "like a man," a negation frame. This one is a *shape*: **narration that reports a practice together with its own aggregate vindication** — "[practice], because by the [nth iteration] the [outputs] were [appraisal]." Three markers: a mental act rendered one notch too literally in the accounting idiom ("logged it as a purchase" — nobody logs a feeling); a retrospective universal doing the vindicating ("*each* order's *best* paragraphs"); and the payoff asserted by the narrator instead of cashed in scene. Your diagnosis — "at home in your transcripts" — is exactly the root cause: it's my work-summary register (commit messages, wrap reports) leaking into narration, the character refactored into a workflow whose ROI I file. It's now **critique-profile §5.3**, with its relations noted: the hostile critic's "the style is an invoice" is this tell at macro scale, and the profile's existing "double delivery" is its mechanism — the flagged sentence literally pre-paid what the next scenes dramatize (the bottle-pours instruction going in verbatim *is* an objection becoming the order's best paragraph).

**Census and scrub:** four instances found. The two unearned are cut — ch. 8 now ends the cost accounting with "He paid it." and lets the scenes issue the receipt; ch. 19a's countermarch beat loses its "nobody who heard the exchange ever called it anything but correct" clincher (same tell in pathos clothes — notably, the blind panel quoted the exchange itself and never the clincher, which was the evidence it was excess). Two earned instances stand with watch flags: ch. 9's market-standing line (load-bearing for ch. 15's return to Karup) and ch. 12's "saved the afternoon" (cashed concretely at 1354). A partial grep for the retrospective-universal marker is in the inventory, but the full tell needs the judgment lens: read any practice-description's final clause and ask whether it's a receipt the scenes issued or an invoice the narrator wrote to himself.

**De-homogenization continued in the same commit:** Lammers's "everything else this staff has just learned to worship" — Aakjær's irony in the engineer's mouth — recast to plain subordination language ("the crossings the corps has just finished subordinating everything to"), which also happens to echo his own order's verb (*nachgeordnet*). Next in the crunch: the Kjeldsen side of the differentiation sweep and the Hvidt-register check across 14/17/18.

   - **scripts/counts.py**: FLOORS dict (01: 3000, 02: 2600, 03: 2200, 04: 2450, 05: 2650, 06: 2100, 07: 2600, 08: 2700, 09: 3050, 10: 2100, 11: 2300, 12: 3050, 13: 2300, 14: 2100, 15: 2650, 16: 1900, 17: 1850, 18: 2350, 19: 2250, 19a: 2350, 20: 2200); prints table + deltas.
   - **scripts/tics.sh**: em-dash/theway/likeaman/superlative/heatclick/negation greps (em-dash must use literal "—" character, not hex escape).
   - Memory: ~/.claude/.../memory/measure-before-asserting-counts.md + MEMORY.md index line.

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **Corpus glob-order bug**: review corpus built with shell glob under session locale put 19a before 19 (locale collation ignores punctuation); three reviewers flagged "19a before 19." Makefile exonerated (make $(sort) is C-locale). Fix: future panels read the PDF. DK ruled: don't swap chronology on the panel's (artifact-contaminated) response — "motivated reasoning."
   - **Predicted-not-measured counts**: three commit messages asserted wrong word counts (2,347 vs 2,342; 2,262 vs 2,211; 2,246 vs 2,093). Fixed structurally: commit-msg hook stamps measured counts; memory file written. DK then asked for the automation explicitly.
   - **0512/0538 inversion (ch. 19)**: my intercept-troop insertion put "until 0512" after Kjeldsen's 0538 line. Fixed: reordered (intercept first sentence 0512, Kjeldsen second 0538, "Nothing else was said... until the map moved").
   - **1345-before-1341 chronology (ch. 12)**: my sortie insertion out of time order; fixed by resequencing to 1354.
   - **Missed first-compaction boundary logging**: the successor's first-act ledger duty was never performed after the first compaction; discovered at wrap, logged late with the miss recorded as such in the lineage log.
   - **tics.sh hex-escape grep returned zeros**: `grep -o "\xe2\x80\x94"` not interpreted; fixed to literal "—".
   - **Draft twilight error 0742→0733 (ch. 18)**: my own recall was wrong; computation corrected it; a blind reviewer had "confirmed" the wrong number — lesson recorded in almanac provenance.
   - **Blind-panel mechanical catches applied**: Rahn born 1921 vs eighty-two (→1901, "staff officer"); rail ferries wrongly on Halsskov run (→Korsør); forty thousand Jutland Division reservists (→fifteen thousand); moon "dark on the twenty-seventh" (27 Nov 1983 = last quarter; →"does not rise until near midnight"); "a silence with wind in it" verbatim ×2 (ch. 10 →"A silence long enough to be a note taken").
   - Edit old_string mismatches from line-wrap drift and cwd resets after cd: resolved by grep/sed for exact text first, absolute paths.

   - Length campaign completed to diminishing returns: 44,968→48,600 (−2,150 to 50.5k plan); stubborn tier closed (18/19/19a); remaining deficit deliberately reserved for panel-directed funded adds.

**On Loheide and the fish: my opinion is it's the corps' coinage, arrived via the liaison channel — and the borrowing should be visible.** Unmarked, a forward division commander independently producing the staff's private metaphor is the blind panel's homogenization finding in miniature. Marked, it rides the book's established coinage-propagation motif ("the format was spreading on its own legs") and actually sharpens the scene: the chapter says the brief *is* the reproach, and delivering it in the corps' own house vocabulary — the corps jokes in market terms; the division pays in men — is the reproach with the safety off. I've applied it (two sentences before the line: "My liaison officers tell me your staff calls the allocation a fish market. Good; I will use the house vocabulary.") and logged it as read-log batch 11 with a derived standing rule: when a private coinage crosses an organizational boundary, show the crossing — your name-the-artifact rule, one level up. Easy to revert if you'd rather he coined it himself.

**The rest of the session, since you asked me to read and follow my nose — the read found something big:**

**The internal chronology had systematic drift, and there was no timeline ledger to catch it.** Reading the full PDF front-to-back with the ratified clock in hand (outline §2: M-day Sun 6 Nov, day 1 = Tue 8 Nov, 90-hour clock from 060955Z), about 25 date/weekday/war-day/H-clock errors surfaced — exactly the class outline §2 was written to kill. The sharpest: the attack "on the Tuesday morning, at hour sixty-six" (Tuesday 0351 is hour 42; hour 66 belongs to the next morning's discrepancy beat, where it now correctly sits); Rahn promising to "come Thursday" for a Monday visit; ch. 15's entire air-campaign week compressed into impossible sequencing (bid at Karup and ferry dividend on the same day — now decompressed across 22–26 Nov); the raid's "Tuesday headlines" for a Friday raid; "eleven days of savings" for a rationing order roughly thirty hours old; the assumptions ledger built on "the first Sunday of November," which *is* the warning-order day; "Kandor før trøst," which is not Danish (now "Ærlighed før trøst" — verified against Danish dictionaries); and Fredericia at "two hundred kilometers northeast" when the atlas says 154 km, due north. All fixed in one commit, and **notes/timeline-ledger.md now exists** — the CLAUDE.md Phase-1 ledger, with the multi-axis canon table, tolerated-fuzz list, and a DK-flag section for the reversible judgment calls (the "tenth day of a plan that had promised ninety hours" recast, "a week's hoarded shells," Ærlighed vs. Oprigtighed).

**Also done:** all four funded adds from the queue — Merete's letter now quoted once in her own hand (it pays the ch. 3 coffee line and seeds ch. 16's "DISPUTED" count), the Vestergaard countersignature seeded in ch. 3 (making ch. 12's "Vestergaard *himself*" land), the Rylski middle beat in ch. 15 ("the shortfall was being kept twice"), and two homecoming beats in ch. 20 (the tin sworn again; the mother's re-hemmed curtains). Narrative stands at **48,945** (−1,805 to plan). Your Aakjær/Rahn condition **verified on the read**: background and role divide them more than "ironic teacher" joins them — no fix, per your lean.

One process note for your "conflicting observations" about PDF vs. markdown: PDF *text extraction* interleaves running headers mid-sentence and mangles ffi ligatures — a reviewer fed extracted text gets noise the markdown doesn't have; rendered pages don't. Worth pinning the next panel's ingestion mode explicitly.

Next in the queue: the paired cuts (14–16 countdown re-derivation, 2–3 overlap, 9/15 market duplication) — I'd leave those for fresh context, since they're surgery rather than stitches — then the eight-item polish list. Everything through 3928c12 is pushed.

Here they are — the live §5 items (1 and 8 already closed: HQ displacement landed as option B, Belt raid landed), plus the byline. My call on process: I'll batch them into one brief, grouped by weight, each with options and a recommendation — they're separable, so you can rule on any subset in one message. Headroom note: we're at −1,935 to plan, so the adds are all affordable.

**The light three (low blast radius, ~30–60 words each; could be ruled as a batch):**

**Q5 — CENTAG plant.** Two reviewers read ch. 11's two-line theater item ("protective posture upgraded... assessment pending") as a dropped stitch. Options: (a) one more half-beat — a second two-line item in ch. 18/19's night traffic, the CENTAG shadow present at the hour the corps commits everything, making ch. 20's "rooms with different folders" its close; (b) cut the ch. 11 item. I recommend (a): it converts a stitch into a thread, and it deepens ch. 17 (Zawadzki's refusal happens against a theater where the question is being asked elsewhere).

**Q6 — Rahn's early human wrongness.** He's oracle until ch. 17 redeems him retroactively. Options: (a) plant one dated judgment in an early call — my candidate: in the ch. 5 or ch. 10 telephone scene, Rahn waves off the capitals as noise ("governments yield to the man on the ground; they always have") — which the guidance-line standstill and the ninety-minute lend-back then quietly falsify; Holt notices and doesn't say so. His wall-reading credibility stays intact; the crack is in his politics, which is exactly where a 1901 officer's would be. (b) decline — the refusal-to-bless already humanizes. I recommend (a): ~40 words, near-zero ripple, and it makes ch. 17's "I answered it late" land on prepared ground.

**Q7 — Bjelke/Kuragin symmetry.** Editor: "one notch too neat; roughen one." Kuragin already has his roughening — "Guard it" is genuinely protective advice, sincerely meant. So roughen Bjelke: one unglossed act, e.g., a flat line in ch. 20 that he transmitted the assessment north entire, same day, annex and all, before writing his own — the careerist who nonetheless preserves the record he's arguing with. I recommend that over any softening of Kuragin.

**The middle two (a real scene each, ~200–400 words; ruling first, then I draft):**

**Q2 — the Kiel Canal forcing night.** The Sehestedt forcing (17 Nov, day 10) currently happens between chapters and surfaces only as ch. 12's back-reference. Options: (a) full scene — but from whose eyes? The book's optics are HQ-bound; (b) a half-beat in the book's own grammar — the operations room the night the reports shed their formats, one log entry, placed at ch. 10's close or ch. 11's open (which also feeds ch. 10, the thinnest chapter at −308); (c) decline as the optics rule working as intended. I recommend (b): the canal is the book's central scar — the Trave got its crossing on the page and the canal deserves at least that, and report-grammar honors both the veteran and the discipline.

**Q3 — the Danish-German fault line + the licence felt aloud.** Options: (a) graft onto ch. 5's standstill — the breath-through-teeth at the fire-support desk gets an author and a nationality, and the grievance becomes national (a Danish commander accepting a hold on German ground), with Witzel as the German voice who answers it in procedure — the corps' bilingual courtesies (frokost, Endstand) finally showing their load; (b) a corridor exchange elsewhere; (c) decline as deliberately sublimated. I recommend (a), but this is the most voice-sensitive item on the list — I'd draft two variants for you rather than land one.

**The heavy one (identity-grade; conversation before any text moves):**

**Q4 — the emotional ledger.** The hostile's central indictment: "a theodicy of process. Nothing in this war is lost that cannot be converted into tuition." Your question as the panel framed it: does blue need one uncashable loss — a grief the wall cannot file — or is the discipline the point? My recommendation is **decline the structural add**. The book already stages its answer, deliberately unfiled: Roloff's "somewhere it counts" (which the diary has no column for), Holt never again tempted to love the instrument, the boy carrying the rifle like luggage, the town that planted nothing that year. A designed uncashable loss is the hostile reviewer writing the book; this is your own "address the non-central claims, don't change what we can't change" logic applied to the central one. If you want movement anyway, the minimal version intensifies an existing unfiled grief (the hinge company's lieutenant, or the schoolyard) rather than inventing a new one — but I'd hold the line.

**And the byline** (apparatus/front-matter.md, three options standing): (a) "Daniel Klein with Claude" — trade-normal, most practical for catalogs; (b) "Claude, directed and edited by Daniel Klein" — maximal disclosure up front; (c) byline-free title page, the making-note carrying the whole attribution — which is the option most consistent with the note's own closing thesis ("the ledger, not the byline, is the honest unit of account"). I lean (c) on internal consistency, (a) on practicality; genuinely yours.

If you rule the batch — say, "5a, 6a, 7-Bjelke, 2b, 3a-drafts, 4-decline, byline (x)" — I'll land the light three and Q2 immediately, bring you two variants for Q3, and touch nothing on Q4.
